[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess… it’s kind of embarrassing. I’ve been helping with the safety trainings at work lately, pointing things out on the floor and in the materials, and I keep noticing my hands. Like how wrinkly they look, and uneven somehow, especially when people are watching.\n\nAnd then they used a photo of me in the newsletter—well, not just me, it was a group thing—and I started thinking my face looked crooked in it. People tell me they don’t really see what I’m talking about, but I can’t seem to stop noticing it. It’s been bothering me more than I expected.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your hands and face looking uneven or crooked when you're alone, or is it mostly when other people are around?\n\nPatient: It’s… well, I notice it most when people are around, I think. Like during the safety trainings, when I’m pointing at something and everyone’s looking, I suddenly can’t stop seeing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. \n\nBut then later I’ll think about it again, especially with that newsletter photo where my face looked crooked to me. Other people say they don’t see it, but I keep going back to it in my head. I guess it started with the trainings and being seen like that.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your hands and face that you notice looking uneven or crooked, or have you had similar thoughts about other parts of your body?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s my hands and my face. I mean, the hands really started it because of the safety trainings—pointing at things, everyone looking, and I’d notice how wrinkly or kind of uneven they seemed. \n\nThen with that newsletter photo, I got stuck on my face looking crooked, even though people kept saying it looked normal. I don’t know, I’ve always hated those work newsletters anyway, they never pick good pictures of anybody… but yes, it’s mainly those two things.\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about your hands and face affected your ability to do your job or interact with others?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t stopped me from doing my job, no. I still go in, still help with the trainings when they ask me to. I’ve always been the kind of person who shows up, you know—my dad was like that too, never missed a day unless he absolutely had to.\n\nBut I am slower getting ready for those trainings now. I’ll fuss with how my hands look, or think about whether people are going to notice. And with photos… I’ve started sort of ducking out of them if I can. Even social things, like little gatherings I used to just go to without thinking, I hesitate more now. Not every time, but enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something that could be done to fix how your hands and face look that would make you stop worrying about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s some “fix,” exactly. I mean, that’s part of why I came in, I guess — since I started helping with those safety trainings, I can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out. They look wrinkly and uneven to me, and then I get thinking about my face in those newsletter photos, like it looks crooked, even though everyone says they don’t see it.\n\nIt’s funny, those newsletters always use the worst pictures — not just of me, of everyone — but still, I keep looking at mine and thinking something is off. So… I don’t know if fixing it would make me stop worrying, but I do feel like if they didn’t look that way to me, maybe I wouldn’t get so stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are focused on specific flaws that you believe are there, regardless of what others say?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it feels like there are specific things I’m seeing, even if other people say they don’t notice. With my hands, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop seeing how wrinkly and uneven they look when I’m pointing at things.\n\nAnd then the newsletter photos—those always bother me, honestly, they never pick flattering ones, but with mine I keep thinking my face looks crooked. People say, “No, it looks fine,” but I still see it. It’s hard not to.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these concerns about your hands and face are affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe. I wouldn’t say it’s ruined anything with my family or friends. I still go to work, and I still help out with the trainings when they need me—though I take longer getting ready for those now, fussing over my hands and all that.\n\nWith friends, it’s more like… if there’s going to be pictures, I try to step out of them, or I’ll hang back. And sometimes before a get-together I hesitate more than I used to. I used to just go, not think twice. My sister always says, “Oh, nobody’s looking at that,” and maybe she’s right, but I still get stuck on it. So it affects things some, but I’m not totally avoiding people.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about your hands and face once you've started noticing something about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s the hard part. Once I notice it, it kind of… sticks. Like with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing at something and then I’m not thinking about the training as much as I’m thinking, “Do my hands look wrinkly? Do they look uneven?” And then later I’ll remember it again.\n\nAnd the newsletter photos, too — I know those pictures are never great, nobody likes their work photos, really, but I’ll see mine and start thinking my face looks crooked. Other people say they don’t see it, and I try to let that settle it, but it doesn’t really stop the thought. It keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are reasonable, or do they seem excessive or irrational to you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that. Part of me thinks, well, I’m seeing what I’m seeing, you know? But then I also know other people don’t seem to react the way I do.\n\nI guess “reasonable” is hard, because everybody worries about how they look a little. My friend at work won’t let anyone take her picture unless she checks her hair first—she’s been like that for years. But with me, once I notice it, I do feel like I get more caught up than I probably should.\n\nSo… maybe it’s excessive sometimes? I don’t know if I’d call it irrational exactly. It feels real to me in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries about your hands and face are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… distress, yes, but not like I’ve stopped my whole life over it. I still go to work, and I still volunteer for the trainings when they ask me. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut it bothers me more than I’d like. Like getting ready for those trainings takes longer now, because I’ll keep looking at my hands and fussing with how they look. And if somebody wants a group photo, I’ll kind of drift to the side or try not to be in it. I didn’t used to think about that so much.\n\nEven with seeing friends, sometimes I’ll hesitate before going, and then I think, well, if I don’t go, then I’ll just sit at home thinking about it anyway. So I usually still go, but it’s not as easy or enjoyable as it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate on your work or daily tasks because of your concerns about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, I still get my work done, and I haven’t stopped doing the trainings or anything. But before a training, I can get caught up getting ready—checking my hands, trying to make them look… I don’t know, not so noticeable—and then I’m slower than I should be.\n\nAt work itself, it’s more like it distracts me in little pieces. Like if there’s a photo being taken, I’ll start thinking about how to avoid it or stand off to the side. And social things too—I’ll hesitate before going sometimes, even if it’s something I used to just enjoy without making a whole production out of it. So it hasn’t stopped me, but it does make things harder than they used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social or work activities that you've completely stopped doing because of your concerns about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped? No, I don’t think so. I still go to work, and I still do the volunteer trainings when they come up.\n\nI mean, I’ll take longer getting ready for those, which is annoying—my sister used to joke I could be ready in ten minutes, though that was years ago and she’s always exaggerating. And now I’ll fuss more, especially with my hands, before I leave.\n\nBut no, I haven’t quit anything outright. I do kind of duck out of photos now, or stand off to the side, and sometimes before seeing people I’ll hesitate and think about not going. Usually I still go, though. It’s just… it takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your hands and face when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s actually kind of why I came in. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be trying to focus on what I’m supposed to be showing people, like pointing something out, and then I suddenly notice my hands — how wrinkly they look, or uneven — and it just sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then with my face, it’s more when those newsletter photos come around. I’ll be trying to just read the newsletter or see who’s in it, and then I’m looking at myself thinking my face looks crooked. Other people say they don’t see it, but I keep seeing it anyway. It’s hard to just let it go once I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are mostly triggered by being in social situations or being seen by others?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, I think it’s worse when I know people can see me. Like with the safety trainings, because I’m pointing things out, I suddenly can’t stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look.\n\nAnd then, you know, the newsletter photos—those really set me off too. I’ll see my face and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. My sister says photos are just bad sometimes, and she’s probably right, but… I don’t know, once I notice it, I keep looking. So yes, being seen definitely makes it harder.\n\nDoctor: Are your concerns about your hands and face interfering with your ability to form or maintain close relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s stopped me from being close to people, exactly. I mean, I still see people, still go to work, still do the volunteer trainings when they come up.\n\nBut I guess it does make things harder in a smaller way. Like before a training I’ll take longer getting ready, fussing with how I look, and then I’m already kind of worn out before I even get there. And with photos, I’ve started trying to duck out of them, which my sister notices—she’s always been the one saying, “Oh, just stand there and smile,” like when we were kids at family picnics, though that’s probably not really what you asked.\n\nSo… close relationships, maybe not directly. I haven’t lost anyone over it. But I do hesitate before social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much, and that probably gets in the way a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about how you can fix or improve the appearance of your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think about it. Not like… big surgery plans or anything, but before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll keep trying to make it better somehow.\n\nI’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands so maybe they don’t look so dry or wrinkly, turn my face different ways to see if it looks uneven. I don’t usually skip the thing, I just… take longer fussing than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the flaws in your hands and face that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure they can, exactly. People keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about, especially with my face in those newsletter photos. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So I usually still go, but it’s not as easy or enjoyable as it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate on your work or daily tasks because of your concerns about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, I still get my work done, and I haven’t stopped doing the trainings or anything. But before a training, I can get caught up getting ready—checking my hands, trying to make them look… I don’t know, not so noticeable—and then I’m slower than I should be.\n\nAt work itself, it’s more like it distracts me in little pieces. Like if there’s a photo being taken, I’ll start thinking about how to avoid it or stand off to the side. And social things too—I’ll hesitate before going sometimes, even if it’s something I used to just enjoy without making a whole production out of it. So it hasn’t stopped me, but it does make things harder than they used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social or work activities that you've completely stopped doing because of your concerns about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped? No, I don’t think so. I still go to work, and I still do the volunteer trainings when they come up.\n\nI mean, I’ll take longer getting ready for those, which is annoying—my sister used to joke I could be ready in ten minutes, though that was years ago and she’s always exaggerating. And now I’ll fuss more, especially with my hands, before I leave.\n\nBut no, I haven’t quit anything outright. I do kind of duck out of photos now, or stand off to the side, and sometimes before seeing people I’ll hesitate and think about not going. Usually I still go, though. It’s just… it takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your hands and face when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s actually kind of why I came in. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be trying to focus on what I’m supposed to be showing people, like pointing something out, and then I suddenly notice my hands — how wrinkly they look, or uneven — and it just sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then with my face, it’s more when those newsletter photos come around. I’ll be trying to just read the newsletter or see who’s in it, and then I’m looking at myself thinking my face looks crooked. Other people say they don’t see it, but I keep seeing it anyway. It’s hard to just let it go once I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are mostly triggered by being in social situations or being seen by others?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, I think it’s worse when I know people can see me. Like with the safety trainings, because I’m pointing things out, I suddenly can’t stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look.\n\nAnd then, you know, the newsletter photos—those really set me off too. I’ll see my face and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. My sister says photos are just bad sometimes, and she’s probably right, but… I don’t know, once I notice it, I keep looking. So yes, being seen definitely makes it harder.\n\nDoctor: Are your concerns about your hands and face interfering with your ability to form or maintain close relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s stopped me from being close to people, exactly. I mean, I still see people, still go to work, still do the volunteer trainings when they come up.\n\nBut I guess it does make things harder in a smaller way. Like before a training I’ll take longer getting ready, fussing with how I look, and then I’m already kind of worn out before I even get there. And with photos, I’ve started trying to duck out of them, which my sister notices—she’s always been the one saying, “Oh, just stand there and smile,” like when we were kids at family picnics, though that’s probably not really what you asked.\n\nSo… close relationships, maybe not directly. I haven’t lost anyone over it. But I do hesitate before social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much, and that probably gets in the way a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about how you can fix or improve the appearance of your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think about it. Not like… big surgery plans or anything, but before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll keep trying to make it better somehow.\n\nI’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands so maybe they don’t look so dry or wrinkly, turn my face different ways to see if it looks uneven. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” which I know probably gets old for them.\n\nIt can make me run a little behind, honestly. But I still get myself there. I don’t usually skip the thing, I just… take longer fussing than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the flaws in your hands and face that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure they can, exactly. People keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about, especially with my face in those newsletter photos. They’ll say it looks fine, or it’s just the angle.\n\nBut when I’m up there doing the safety trainings and pointing at things, I feel like my hands are right there for everyone to notice—how wrinkly they look, uneven. I came in because I just can’t seem to stop seeing it myself. So… maybe other people don’t see it the way I do, but it feels like they must.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Maybe… yes, a little. I mean, I know people keep saying they don’t see it, so part of me thinks maybe I’m making it bigger than it is.

Like during the safety trainings, when I’m pointing at something and everyone’s looking, I suddenly can’t stop seeing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look. \n\nBut then later I’ll think about it again, especially with that newsletter photo where my face looked crooked to me. Other people say they don’t see it, but I keep going back to it in my head. I guess it started with the trainings and being seen like that.\n\nDoctor: Is it always your hands and face that you notice looking uneven or crooked, or have you had similar thoughts about other parts of your body?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s my hands and my face. I mean, the hands really started it because of the safety trainings—pointing at things, everyone looking, and I’d notice how wrinkly or kind of uneven they seemed. \n\nThen with that newsletter photo, I got stuck on my face looking crooked, even though people kept saying it looked normal. I don’t know, I’ve always hated those work newsletters anyway, they never pick good pictures of anybody… but yes, it’s mainly those two things.\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about your hands and face affected your ability to do your job or interact with others?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t stopped me from doing my job, no. I still go in, still help with the trainings when they ask me to. I’ve always been the kind of person who shows up, you know—my dad was like that too, never missed a day unless he absolutely had to.\n\nBut I am slower getting ready for those trainings now. I’ll fuss with how my hands look, or think about whether people are going to notice. And with photos… I’ve started sort of ducking out of them if I can. Even social things, like little gatherings I used to just go to without thinking, I hesitate more now. Not every time, but enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something that could be done to fix how your hands and face look that would make you stop worrying about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s some “fix,” exactly. I mean, that’s part of why I came in, I guess — since I started helping with those safety trainings, I can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out. They look wrinkly and uneven to me, and then I get thinking about my face in those newsletter photos, like it looks crooked, even though everyone says they don’t see it.\n\nIt’s funny, those newsletters always use the worst pictures — not just of me, of everyone — but still, I keep looking at mine and thinking something is off. So… I don’t know if fixing it would make me stop worrying, but I do feel like if they didn’t look that way to me, maybe I wouldn’t get so stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are focused on specific flaws that you believe are there, regardless of what others say?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it feels like there are specific things I’m seeing, even if other people say they don’t notice. With my hands, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop seeing how wrinkly and uneven they look when I’m pointing at things.\n\nAnd then the newsletter photos—those always bother me, honestly, they never pick flattering ones, but with mine I keep thinking my face looks crooked. People say, “No, it looks fine,” but I still see it. It’s hard not to.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these concerns about your hands and face are affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe. I wouldn’t say it’s ruined anything with my family or friends. I still go to work, and I still help out with the trainings when they need me—though I take longer getting ready for those now, fussing over my hands and all that.\n\nWith friends, it’s more like… if there’s going to be pictures, I try to step out of them, or I’ll hang back. And sometimes before a get-together I hesitate more than I used to. I used to just go, not think twice. My sister always says, “Oh, nobody’s looking at that,” and maybe she’s right, but I still get stuck on it. So it affects things some, but I’m not totally avoiding people.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about your hands and face once you've started noticing something about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s the hard part. Once I notice it, it kind of… sticks. Like with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing at something and then I’m not thinking about the training as much as I’m thinking, “Do my hands look wrinkly? Do they look uneven?” And then later I’ll remember it again.\n\nAnd the newsletter photos, too — I know those pictures are never great, nobody likes their work photos, really, but I’ll see mine and start thinking my face looks crooked. Other people say they don’t see it, and I try to let that settle it, but it doesn’t really stop the thought. It keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are reasonable, or do they seem excessive or irrational to you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that. Part of me thinks, well, I’m seeing what I’m seeing, you know? But then I also know other people don’t seem to react the way I do.\n\nI guess “reasonable” is hard, because everybody worries about how they look a little. My friend at work won’t let anyone take her picture unless she checks her hair first—she’s been like that for years. But with me, once I notice it, I do feel like I get more caught up than I probably should.\n\nSo… maybe it’s excessive sometimes? I don’t know if I’d call it irrational exactly. It feels real to me in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries about your hands and face are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… distress, yes, but not like I’ve stopped my whole life over it. I still go to work, and I still volunteer for the trainings when they ask me. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nBut it bothers me more than I’d like. Like getting ready for those trainings takes longer now, because I’ll keep looking at my hands and fussing with how they look. And if somebody wants a group photo, I’ll kind of drift to the side or try not to be in it. I didn’t used to think about that so much.\n\nEven with seeing friends, sometimes I’ll hesitate before going, and then I think, well, if I don’t go, then I’ll just sit at home thinking about it anyway. So I usually still go, but it’s not as easy or enjoyable as it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate on your work or daily tasks because of your concerns about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, I still get my work done, and I haven’t stopped doing the trainings or anything. But before a training, I can get caught up getting ready—checking my hands, trying to make them look… I don’t know, not so noticeable—and then I’m slower than I should be.\n\nAt work itself, it’s more like it distracts me in little pieces. Like if there’s a photo being taken, I’ll start thinking about how to avoid it or stand off to the side. And social things too—I’ll hesitate before going sometimes, even if it’s something I used to just enjoy without making a whole production out of it. So it hasn’t stopped me, but it does make things harder than they used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social or work activities that you've completely stopped doing because of your concerns about your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped? No, I don’t think so. I still go to work, and I still do the volunteer trainings when they come up.\n\nI mean, I’ll take longer getting ready for those, which is annoying—my sister used to joke I could be ready in ten minutes, though that was years ago and she’s always exaggerating. And now I’ll fuss more, especially with my hands, before I leave.\n\nBut no, I haven’t quit anything outright. I do kind of duck out of photos now, or stand off to the side, and sometimes before seeing people I’ll hesitate and think about not going. Usually I still go, though. It’s just… it takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your hands and face when you're trying to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s actually kind of why I came in. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be trying to focus on what I’m supposed to be showing people, like pointing something out, and then I suddenly notice my hands — how wrinkly they look, or uneven — and it just sticks in my head.\n\nAnd then with my face, it’s more when those newsletter photos come around. I’ll be trying to just read the newsletter or see who’s in it, and then I’m looking at myself thinking my face looks crooked. Other people say they don’t see it, but I keep seeing it anyway. It’s hard to just let it go once I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face are mostly triggered by being in social situations or being seen by others?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, I think it’s worse when I know people can see me. Like with the safety trainings, because I’m pointing things out, I suddenly can’t stop noticing how wrinkly and uneven my hands look.\n\nAnd then, you know, the newsletter photos—those really set me off too. I’ll see my face and think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. My sister says photos are just bad sometimes, and she’s probably right, but… I don’t know, once I notice it, I keep looking. So yes, being seen definitely makes it harder.\n\nDoctor: Are your concerns about your hands and face interfering with your ability to form or maintain close relationships?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s stopped me from being close to people, exactly. I mean, I still see people, still go to work, still do the volunteer trainings when they come up.\n\nBut I guess it does make things harder in a smaller way. Like before a training I’ll take longer getting ready, fussing with how I look, and then I’m already kind of worn out before I even get there. And with photos, I’ve started trying to duck out of them, which my sister notices—she’s always been the one saying, “Oh, just stand there and smile,” like when we were kids at family picnics, though that’s probably not really what you asked.\n\nSo… close relationships, maybe not directly. I haven’t lost anyone over it. But I do hesitate before social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much, and that probably gets in the way a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about how you can fix or improve the appearance of your hands and face?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think about it. Not like… big surgery plans or anything, but before a class or one of those senior-center workshops, I’ll keep trying to make it better somehow.\n\nI’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands so maybe they don’t look so dry or wrinkly, turn my face different ways to see if it looks uneven. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look all right?” which I know probably gets old for them.\n\nIt can make me run a little behind, honestly. But I still get myself there. I don’t usually skip the thing, I just… take longer fussing than I mean to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the flaws in your hands and face that you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure they can, exactly. People keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about, especially with my face in those newsletter photos. They’ll say it looks fine, or it’s just the angle.\n\nBut when I’m up there doing the safety trainings and pointing at things, I feel like my hands are right there for everyone to notice—how wrinkly they look, uneven. I came in because I just can’t seem to stop seeing it myself. So… maybe other people don’t see it the way I do, but it feels like they must.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hands and face might be exaggerated or not based in reality?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, a little. I mean, I know people keep saying they don’t see it, so part of me thinks maybe I’m making it bigger than it is.\n\nBut since I started helping with those safety trainings, I just can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly they look, kind of uneven. And then in the newsletter photos I start thinking my face looks crooked, even though other people say, “No, that looks fine.”\n\nSo I suppose it might be exaggerated, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hands and face actually look uneven or crooked, regardless of what others tell you?\n\nPatient: I… I think they do, yes, at least when I’m looking at them. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t seem to stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing at things—like they look wrinkly, uneven, just not right.\n\nAnd then those newsletter photos, my face looks crooked to me. Other people say they don’t see it, and maybe they’re just being kind, you know? My sister does that sometimes too, not about this exactly, but she’ll tell me “it’s fine” when she doesn’t want me to worry. So I don’t know… but to me, yes, it really does look that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
